FOREWORD

to “50 Years of Best Sellers”

The term “best seller” was coined and came into common use because it

filled a need. A term was needed to describe what were not necessarily

the best books but the books that people liked best. For a long time

people have been interested in what other people read and why. If we are

studying what books were popular in any period of our history, it is

important to know not only what people could have read, or should have

read, but what they did read. The record of what books a great many

people have bought and read is a part of the social history of those

people.

The books which have been important from a literary standpoint have been

recorded in many volumes of literary criticism. Many of those books have

had large and continuing sales, though some have only been appreciated

years after they were published. These great books are the product in

which any country must take great pride. On the other hand the record of

best sellers of the hundred or more years during which book publishing

has been an active industry in this country has not heretofore been

adequately made. The record is only in part literary history. A literary

classic is a book which continues to be important to succeeding

generations of readers. Some of the best sellers of each decade have

done that; many more have not. The lists covering a fifty-year period

which this volume includes will make interesting material for study.

Whether or not literature of continuing value is to be found among the

best sellers, the lists provide significant insight into the thinking

and emotions of a people. The trends of such reading may have more

significance than a casual glance indicates.

How many copies any book has sold is recorded on the files of its

publisher, and business records are not readily unearthed. Fortunately,

however, the comparative sales of books year by year for the last half

century have been kept systematically. The closing of this fifty year

period makes a natural occasion for printing the record. The sales

totals of books are more difficult to gather and to verify, but such

figures, insofar as they have ever been printed, have become part of the

historical files of the _Publishers’ Weekly_ office. Alice Payne

Hackett, who has had so much to do with the development and completeness

of these files, has pursued for her book every figure that could be

verified by the files or records in the publishing offices.

_Fifty Years of Best Sellers_ will open up to the student and bookman

new fields for speculation. Perhaps students of literary history will

try to chart the evolution of public taste from these records and

writers of tomorrow’s best sellers find, in these titles of the past,

some clue to the perennial elements of popular appeal.

FREDERIC G. MELCHER

     1946

FOREWORD

to “60 Years of Best Sellers”

The prompt recognition which was accorded ten years ago to Alice

Hackett’s records and comments on best sellers and the demand which has

followed for a continuation of her figures and analysis has shown that

such records have an interest to widely different groups of people--to

the book trade, of course, but also to reminiscent readers and to

students of public taste and social history. This continuation of the

yearly lists is now accompanied by an expansion of earlier information

which Miss Hackett has gathered by persistent contacts with many sources

and to these annual lists has been added a greatly extended group of

classified lists which enhance the popular interest and usefulness of

the book.

FREDERIC G. MELCHER

     August 1956

KEY TO ENDPAPERS

In the upper lefthand corner are the title page and frontispiece of _The

Royal Road to Romance_, which introduced Richard Halliburton as the

glamorous traveler of the ’20’s. Next is the title page of H. G. Wells’

most famous best seller, _The Outline of History_. To its right, and

just below it, are pictures of the _Complete Poems_ of Robert Frost, one

of our best-loved poets, and of _The White Cliffs_, by Alice Duer

Miller, best-selling poetry of World War II.

At the middle left is Alfred Aloysius Horn, whose fabulous _Trader

Horn_, was a high spot of the ’20’s. Emily Post’s _Etiquette_ in its

original and revised editions has been a steady seller for thirty or

more years. Just below _Etiquette_ is the famous _Story of Philosophy_

by Will Durant, and above it one of the most outstanding religious

novels of our time, _The Robe_, by Lloyd C. Douglas. At the right is an

illustration from one of the earliest best-selling American mystery

stories, _The Circular Staircase_, by the dean of mystery writers, Mary

Roberts Rinehart.

The bottom row includes a picture of the top seller in religious

non-fiction, _The Greatest Story Ever Told_, by Fulton Oursler; the

bestselling novel of this century, _God’s Little Acre_, by Erskine

Caldwell, in its paperbound edition; Dr. Benjamin Spock’s _Pocket Book

of Baby and Child Care_, the overall best seller in paperbound books;

and the title page of the first edition of _John Brown’s Body_, Stephen

Vincent Benet’s great narrative poem.

On the right page, at the upper left, is _The Honorable Peter Stirling_,

by Paul Leicester Ford, a sensation of the 1890’s, followed by the title

page of Margaret Mitchell’s _Gone with the Wind_, and the double title

page of the special pre-publication edition of Edna Ferber’s _Show

Boat_.

Below is the paperbound edition of Erle Stanley Gardner’s first Perry

Mason mystery, _The Case of the Velvet Claws_. In descending order are

Rabbi Liebman’s _Peace of Mind_; _The Good Earth_, flanked by a picture

of its author, Pearl Buck; and _Contract Bridge Complete_, by the

best-selling writer on bridge of the ’50’s, Charles Goren. At the left

is the title page of one of the best-known western stories, _The

Virginian_, by Owen Wister.

Along the lowest row are Dr. Norman Vincent Peale’s _The Power of

Positive Thinking_; the jacket of the original edition of L. Frank

Baum’s _The Wonderful Wizard of Oz_, perennial juvenile favorite; the

paperbound edition of _One Lonely Night_, by the sensational detective

writer of the ’50’s, Mickey Spillane; and the new edition of the

bestseller among cook books, the _Better Homes and Gardens Cook Book_.

A GUIDE TO BEST SELLERS

A GUIDE TO BEST SELLERS

It is the purpose of this book as of its predecessor, _Fifty Years of

Best Sellers_, to present as completely as possible the facts and

figures about American best sellers, to interpret and comment to some

extent upon the statistics presented but not to evaluate from a literary

standpoint.

There is always a great deal of interest and curiosity about

best-selling books among members of the general reading public as well

as among those concerned with the publishing, selling and publicizing of

books. The lists and figures of this book are an attempt to answer the

questions of such interested people. In view of the recurrent

controversies about the best-seller system, which usually hinge upon the

complaint that best seller lists draw attention only to a few books to

the disadvantage of what are possibly more worthy books, it must be

added that this book is not designed to glorify the best seller per se,

but simply to give the facts. As long as newspapers and magazines find

best seller lists attractive features for their readers, the best seller

will be with us. Even if there were no organized best seller lists,

notable sales for a certain book would be news. When a book becomes

conspicuous for its fast and unusually large sales, people who are not

ordinarily book buyers or not particularly interested in books become

aware of it, even buy it. Sales themselves stimulate sales. They cannot

be ignored as a factor in the book business.

The term best seller is usually comparative rather than absolute. A book

may be the best seller in one store or one city; it may be the best

seller over the country for a week, or a year, for five years or for

sixty. The words used to designate such a book have become so

established in our language and are so often loosely used that “best

seller” has too often become synonymous with “book,” especially in

circles outside the book industry. Advertisers, magazine editors, and

program directors in radio and television, attempting to adhere to fair

standards when mentioning books, often query major review media,

libraries, trade magazines, book associations, etc. as to whether a

certain book may honestly be called a best seller. In the office of

_Publishers’ Weekly_, the book trade magazine where much of the material

for this present volume was developed, it has become the practice to

answer such questions in the affirmative if that book has appeared on

one of its monthly best seller lists, which are based upon bookstore

surveys. The book is a best seller for at least the moment at which the

question is asked. But is the book of the moment or month a best seller

as _David Copperfield_ or _Gone with the Wind_ is a best seller? In the

pages following I have attempted to illustrate the various states of

best sellerdom.

The reason why the story of best sellers begins in 1895 is that it was

in that year that the first best seller lists were printed in the United

States. Harry Thurston Peck, then editor of the literary magazine, _The

Bookman_, began to run each month the lists of the best-selling books in

a number of large cities. By 1902, “best seller” had been established,

mainly through the publicity derived from _The Bookman’s_ lists, as a

term specifically applied to books. About 1903 the magazine set the

limit of its monthly list of titles at six and called the lists “the six

best sellers.” From these _Bookman_ lists were formulated the best

seller lists for the years 1895 to 1912 when the _Publishers’ Weekly_

lists were begun. There were few and scattered records of best-selling

books previous to 1895. There are occasional lists in early literary

magazines, some in _Publishers’ Weekly_, a few lists of the most popular

reading matter in public libraries to be found, but all these give the

effect of rather random reporting. Without any methodical listing of

best sellers in the years previous to 1895, it is practically impossible

to arrive at exact figures of sales for the books of preceding centuries

or even at estimates except in the case of the books which have endured.

A list of “Early Best Sellers,” most of which have been read for more

than a century, is included in this book. It has been checked by a

number of authorities, and, while it may possibly not be complete, the

titles on it comprise a valid body of best sellers published before

The first edition of this book covered the years from 1895 to 1945.

Since then there have been tremendous changes in the business of

bookselling, principally in methods of selling. During and just after

World War II more books could have been sold than were available. This

was because of paper quotas, shortages in other materials, and wartime

demands. Children’s books, especially, sold as fast as they could be

placed on sale, for one reason because the metals used in making toys

for children were in even shorter supply than paper. All books sold very

well in spite of limitations placed upon their manufacture. There were

fewer titles published but those that were published and were successful

sold out all their printings. At about the time that paper restrictions

began to be lifted book clubs boomed. The major clubs had been in

existence for a number of years but it was not until the postwar period

that all their monthly selections sold into many hundreds of thousands.

The Book-of-the-Month Club distributed a total of 7,011,936 books in

1950. In its fiscal year of 1950 the Literary Guild sent out about

8,800,000 books. Compare these figures with those estimated by Maxwell

Aley in the June 6, 1931 _Publishers’ Weekly_. He concluded then that

there were “one million habitual book buyers, one million more

occasional buyers and beyond that a vague but large number who buy one

or two books a year.” Because of the popularity of the books in the

Armed Services Editions, distributed to men and women in the Armed

Services during the war, the book trade hoped that the many new readers

of books would continue to read in the postwar years, and that the book

boom of the late ’40’s and early ’50’s would continue.

The big totals piled up by the book clubs had a tremendous effect on

what I have called the overall best seller lists, the three lists that

include books that have sold 750,000 copies or more in the past sixty

years. Many books of the mid-century have displaced the favorites of the

century’s early years that once were high on the lists. The reading

picture given by the list is entirely different from what it was ten

years ago.

At almost the same time as wartime demands were creating new best

sellers, another tremendous change which affected the overall best

seller lists, was taking place in the book business. That was the

success of the 25-cent paperbound book. This was not a new development

in the trade. In the nineteenth century, in the “dime novel” period,

paper bound books had big sales. Dime novels flourished for years. There

were also many experiments in later years to launch paperbound lines,

but it was not until 1939 that the paperbound book came into its own for

moderns. Its success was due to new methods of cheaper manufacture, to

new methods of distribution through the magazine wholesaler system, and

to improved literary quality of the books chosen for reprint in paper

covers.

When the first edition of this book appeared, paperbound books were

established but had not yet attained the enormous sales that came later

on. There was one “overall” best-seller list, 1895-1945, in _Fifty Years

of Best Sellers_. Both hardbound books, including the reprints at 75

cents and $1 which sold well up to the 1940’s, and paperbound books were

lumped together on it. Paperbound sales account for the place of only a

few books on the list--books like _See Here, Private Hargrove_ and _The

Pocket Book of War Humor_. That list, made in 1944, was, on the whole,

not greatly affected by paperbound book sales. Since then the whole

picture has changed enormously. Paperbound sales, both reprints of

hardcover editions and books which have made their first appearance in

paper covers, account for by far the majority of titles.

That is why, for this new edition, I have arranged the overall list,

1895-1955, in three sections. Students of the book business and of

literary taste will find what books have sold to bookstore and book club

customers, what titles have sold to the so-called mass market. The

evidence is not quite what one would expect. While great numbers of

detective and western stories and some sensational fiction on the list

are due to paperbound sales, it is also true that paperbound

distribution accounts for the appearance of such authors as John

Steinbeck and William Faulkner on the overall combined list.

In general the sales figures on these three lists are much larger than

those on the 1945 list. The minimum sales figure on that list was half a

million copies. On two of the 1955 lists the minimum is one million; on

the hardbound list, 750,000. There are also many more titles on these

lists than on the 1945 list. Title production has increased every year

since the end of the war and the reading population has also increased.

While the sales figures for the most popular books are startlingly

larger than those of a decade ago, they are not so great as one must

believe they could be in relation to growth of population and the

increase in high school and college enrollment. Robert Frase, economic

consultant and associate director of the American Book Publishers

Council, has recently pointed out that this population pattern ought

soon to affect the book business favorably even if it has not yet

affected it as much as expected. This slow rate of increase in sales, if

not in title production, is illustrated by figures Mr. Frase has quoted

for trade books sold in 1929 and in 1954. The figures for both years are

practically the same, about 70,000,000 copies. Book club sales, however,

rose from about 2,000,000 to 50,000,000; paperbacks from zero to

190,000,000; juveniles, at all prices, from 37,000,000 to 95,000,000.

Among trade books, the topmost best sellers are selling in even greater

quantities than they did previously, but for the run-of-the-mill books

the situation is not so happy. Big best sellers, textbooks, children’s

books, and other special types of books are going along at a good rate.

The novel or book of non-fiction that may be worthy but is not

particularly outstanding has a rougher road. Because of increasing

manufacturing costs and the decrease in rental libraries, which once

absorbed a creditable number of the ordinary romances and novels

designed for a few hours’ diversion, it is difficult for the publisher

to sell the unassuming book profitably. Contrary to public assumption,

most authors of books do not make much money. They must either write a

great many books or else produce a few big best sellers in order to

attain satisfactory incomes. Laura Z. Hobson, author of _Gentleman’s

Agreement_ and other books, has made the statement that of the 2,000

members of the Authors’ Guild, less than half make an annual income of

$3,000 or more from all forms of writing. But an author may not have to

live on book sales alone. The sales of subsidiary rights, i.e., rights

to use a book as the basis of a movie, television show or condensation,

are becoming of more and more importance both to author and publisher;

but it is mainly the “big” books that bring in the big money, and they

are few and far between. _The Desperate Hours_, for example, brought

its author, Joseph Hayes, a quarter of a million dollars within a very

short time--but this included, besides sales of the book and of book

club editions, the sale of the play and screen rights, _Reader’s Digest_

condensation and magazine serialization. One book that almost reached

the ultimate in all types of sales was _The Razor’s Edge_ by W. Somerset

Maugham. It was serialized in _Redbook_, was a best seller in its trade

edition, was both a Literary Guild and a Dollar Book Club best seller,

sold in a clothbound $1 reprint and in a 25-cent paperbound reprint, and

was produced as a movie. Numerous foreign rights were sold.

Readers may wonder why many, if not most, of the best sellers on the

three overall lists do not appear on the annual lists of best sellers,

1895 through 1955, and, conversely, why many of the titles on the yearly

lists do not appear on the overall best seller lists. This situation

illustrates the difference between the best seller of the year and the

all-time best seller. These annual lists as compiled by the _Publishers’

Weekly_, are primarily for the interest of the retail bookseller. They

do not include book club sales or sales in paperbound reprint editions.

They are intended to show the reading tastes of the book buyers who

actually go to a store to select books, not of those who buy by mail or

pick up random reading from newsstands. Most of the books on the annual

lists of recent years sell a minimum of about 75,000 copies. It takes

several years’ big sales to put one of the books from the yearly lists

on the all-time list. Book club sales and paperbound sales will also

help do the job.

The reason why many of the books on the all-time lists do not appear on

yearly lists is, in general, the same. In the case where book club or

paperbound sales do not enter into the situation the books probably have

sold steadily, piling up sales over the years until they reach big

quantities. This is especially true of the cook books which stand high

on the hardbound list; four out of the first five titles on the list are

cook books.

These yearly lists are more interesting for the student of social

history and literary taste than are the overall lists. They reflect

events and crises in history over sixty years as well as the changing

mores--from Pollyanna to Polly Adler. There are books on these lists

that have made history and influenced American thinking. Consider _The

Honorable Peter Stirling_ by Paul Leicester Ford, _The Pit_ by Frank

Norris, _The Jungle_, by Upton Sinclair, _Mr. Britling Sees It Through_,

by H. G. Wells, _The Economic Consequences of the Peace_ by John Maynard

Keynes, _The Plastic Age_ by Percy Marks, _Mother India_ by Katherine

Mayo, _Looking Forward_ by Franklin D. Roosevelt, _It Can’t Happen Here_

by Sinclair Lewis, _The Grapes of Wrath_ by John Steinbeck, _For Whom

the Bell Tolls_ by Ernest Hemingway, _Berlin Diary_ by William L.

Shirer, _The White Cliffs_ by Alice Duer Miller, _You Can’t Do Business

with Hitler_ by Douglas S. Miller, _Victory Through Air Power_ by Major

Alexander P. de Seversky, _They Were Expendable_ by W. L. White, _One

World_ by Wendell L. Willkie, _Strange Fruit_ by Lillian Smith, _I Chose

Freedom_ by Victor Kravchenko, _Gentleman’s Agreement_ by Laura Z.

Hobson, _Kingsblood Royal_ by Sinclair Lewis, _The Wall_ by John Hersey,

_Witness_ by Whittaker Chambers, _But We Were Born Free_ by Elmer Davis,

_Why Johnny Can’t Read_ by Rudolf Flesch and _Year of Decisions_ by

Harry S. Truman. There are many books which have added new words and

phrases to the American language: _The Virginian_, _Pollyanna_, _Over

the Top_, _Main Street_, _Babbitt_, _Gentlemen Prefer Blondes_, _Back

Street_, _Life Begins at Forty_, _Mr. Chips_, _Lost Horizon_, _The

Hucksters_, _The Snake Pit_, and _The Man in the Gray Flannel Suit_.

Some are known by more people because their titles are the titles of

songs: _The Trail of the Lonesome Pine_, _The Rosary_, _In Flanders

Fields_, _The Sheik_, _Stars Fell on Alabama_, _My Sister and I_, and

_The Last Time I Saw Paris_.

Of interest to publishers is the number of titles on the annual list

that specific firms have issued. The leaders are Doubleday (with

predecessor firms), 89; Harper, 80; Houghton Mifflin, 46; Scribner, 43;

Little, Brown, 41; Bobbs-Merrill (with predecessor firms) and Macmillan,

40 each; Appleton-Century-Crofts (with predecessor firms), 39; Simon and

Schuster, 31; Lippincott (with Stokes), 23; and Dodd Mead, 20. With one

exception all these houses were founded in the nineteenth century. Simon

and Schuster is the youngest, and its 31 best sellers appear only on the

annual lists since 1924. The authors who have had the most titles on the

sixty annual lists are Mary Roberts Rinehart with eleven; Sinclair

Lewis with ten; Zane Grey and Booth Tarkington with nine each; and Louis

Bromfield, Winston Churchill (the American novelist), George Barr

McCutcheon, Gene Stratton Porter, and Frank Yerby with eight each. In

the first twenty years, 1895-1914, the authors who appeared most

frequently were George Barr McCutcheon, with eight titles; Winston

Churchill with seven, and Mary Johnston and Sir Gilbert Parker with five

each. In the period 1915-1934, Zane Grey led with nine titles. He was

followed by Warwick Deeping, Sinclair Lewis, Mary Roberts Rinehart, and

Booth Tarkington, with seven each, and by Coningsby Dawson, Eleanor H.

Porter, Gene Stratton Porter, and H. G. Wells with five each. In the

past twenty years, the authors having most titles on the lists are Frank

Yerby with eight; Daphne du Maurier, John P. Marquand and John Steinbeck

with six each; and Thomas B. Costain, A. J. Cronin, Lloyd C. Douglas,

John Gunther, James Hilton, and Frances Parkinson Keyes with five each.

The authors whose books have covered the longest span on all the lists

are: Ellen Glasgow, whose first best seller appeared on the 1904 list

and her last on the 1935 list; Edna Ferber, 1924 and 1952; Mary Roberts

Rinehart, 1909 and 1936; Frances Hodgson Burnett, 1896 and 1922;

Sinclair Lewis, 1921 and 1947; and Louis Bromfield, 1927 and 1945.

As I have said, literary criticism does not enter into this book; it is

a guide to the past and present, and, for those looking toward future

best sellers, a starting-point for speculation. It is wise to bear in

mind Samuel Butler’s statement: “There are some things which it is

madness not to try to know but where it is almost as much madness to try

to know. Sometimes publishers hoping to buy the Holy Ghost with a price,

fee a man to read for them and advise them. This is but as the vain

tossing of insomnia. God will not have any human being know what will

sell.” Years ago the formula for a best seller was summed up as

Lincoln’s Doctor’s Dog--because books about Lincoln, about doctors and

about dogs are always popular. Today the title might be amended to How

to Be Lincoln’s Doctor’s Dog’s Psychiatrist. Or the writer might follow

the caption for a drawing by Alan Dunn in the _New Yorker_, “It can’t

miss, J. G.! The author got disillusioned with Communism, escaped from

behind the Iron Curtain, came to the United States, lived on a

sharecropper’s farm in Georgia, spent a year in a state insane asylum,

turned to religion, and now is a monk.”

What best sellers of today will become the classics of tomorrow? Malcolm

Cowley has pointed out, in an article in the _New Republic_ (Dec. 22,

1947), “Classics and Best Sellers,” the distinction between a classic (a

book admired by intelligent readers through several generations) and a

best seller (a book purchased by many readers in one generation). The

terms, he says, “are not mutually exclusive--a best seller may become a

classic in time, as happened with Dickens’ novels.” In order to try to

find out how many of the books on the early years’ lists were at all

familiar to today’s young generation, I read the titles and authors’

names to several recent college graduates. Only the most obvious authors

were known to most of them, and extremely few titles. The lists were

also tried out on freshman English composition classes in a New England

college, with equally negative results. I quote from a letter written by

the teacher of the classes. “These students are almost completely

ignorant of the best sellers you have listed. Occasionally the name of

an author may remind them of something they’ve heard their parents

discuss. In some cases, they had heard the name of the book, but knew

nothing of the author, e.g., _Seventeen_, _If Winter Comes_, _Gentlemen

Prefer Blondes_.... Generally the best sellers of yesterday are a dead

issue to the college freshman of today. The one exception is _Gone With

the Wind_ which has had a slight renaissance since the movie was

reissued.... My concern is not that these students are ignorant of the

best sellers of yesteryear, but that most of them know nothing about

contemporary American and English literature. Their ignorance is

appalling! Many have read nothing by Hemingway, Steinbeck, Eugene

O’Neill, Faulkner, Maugham, Hilton, Shaw, etc. A few have read _From

Here to Eternity_ and some are at least familiar with the titles of book

club books that they have seen around their homes. In school they may

have read _Arrowsmith_, but they have never heard of Dreiser, Edith

Wharton, Booth Tarkington, Winston Churchill, Willa Cather, or

practically anyone since Mark Twain.”

To relate the material, the facts and figures of this present book up to

1950, to their historical and sociological background, I heartily

recommend the reading of James D. Hart’s _The Popular Book_ (Oxford

University Press, 1950). For a final word on the whole business of best

sellers I quote from his concluding paragraphs. “If a student of taste

wants to know the thoughts and feelings of the majority who lived during

Franklin Pierce’s administration, he will find more positive value in

Maria Cummins’s _The Lamplighter_ or T. S. Arthur’s _Ten Nights in a

Bar-Room_ than he will in Thoreau’s _Walden_--all books published in

1854. The mores and manners prevailing in the year of the Teapot Dome

scandal can better be extrapolated from Gertrude Atherton’s _Black Oxen_

than from Wallace Stevens’s _Harmonium_. The book that time judges to be

great is occasionally also the book popular in its own period; but, by

and large, the longer-lived work reflects the demands of the moment only

in the most general sense. Usually the book that is popular pleases the

reader because it is shaped by the same forces that mold his non-reading

hours, so that its dispositions and convictions, its language and

subject, re-create the sense of the present, to die away as soon as that

present becomes the past. Books of that sort generally are unreadable

for succeeding ages; but like other fragments of the past, they help

form the present. The volumes themselves may gather dust on library

shelves, but they have left lasting impression on the American mind,

etched deeply into a national consciousness.”

BEST SELLERS

1895-1955

This overall list of best sellers includes books published in the United

States from 1895 through 1955, which have, over sixty years, sold one

million copies or more. Most of them have appeared in many different

editions and in both hardbound and paperbound format. Many have been

sold by book clubs, on newsstands, and by mail as well as through

bookstores. Most of the titles, with some notable exceptions, represent

the most popular reading habit, that is, reading for entertainment.

Pamphlets are not included in the list, nor are dictionaries,

encyclopedias, atlases, editions of the Bible, hymnals, prayer books,

manuals, textbooks, and certain other books, such as game and novelty

books. Best sellers in these specialized fields are, in general, covered

under other headings. Children’s books are included when they are

primarily juvenile novels and not picture or game books.

Publishers’ names are not given because most of the books have been

published by at least two firms and sometimes by five or six or even

more. The date of original publication in this country follows the

author’s name. “F” indicates fiction, “J” juveniles. Many of the titles

listed as juveniles were originally published as adult books, but

changing taste has now definitely placed them in the category of

juvenile reading, and they are so indicated in accordance with modern

standards.

Three hundred and six titles make up this list of books which have sold

over 1,000,000 copies in the years since 1895. In the original edition

of the present book a somewhat comparable list appeared, an overall

best-seller list of books which had sold 500,000 copies or more in the

years from 1895 through 1945. There were only 143 titles on that list.

The enormous increase in both number of best-selling books, and in sales

is due not only to the normal increase in the number of new titles

issued in the years since 1945 but in much greater part to the increase

in the production and distribution of paperbound books and to the big

jump in book club sales during the years immediately after World War II.

This revolution in the make-up of the overall best-seller list has

resulted in the elimination of most of the popular books that first

appeared in the years up to 1930. There are only 14 titles on the list

that were published in the years between 1916 and 1930. Some of the most

popular authors of the early part of this century like the novelist

Winston Churchill, whose historical tales were the best sellers of

almost every year from 1901 through 1915, and Harold Bell Wright and

Gene Stratton Porter, whose names predominated on the earlier list, have

all but disappeared from this one. On the other hand, the paperback

editions are responsible for the inclusion on this list of many of the

books of twenty or so years ago which never had extremely large sales in

their hardback editions--books by authors like Erskine Caldwell, Thorne

Smith, William Faulkner, and Paul De Kruif. It is the paperbacks, too,

that have created the large sales for the many detective and western

stories that appear here. _In His Steps_, _Gone with the Wind_, and _How

to Win Friends and Influence People_ are still right at the top. Some of

the older favorites are still here, but much farther down the list than

they were, such titles as _Pollyanna_, _The Rosary_, _The Little

Shepherd of Kingdom Come_, and _The Sheik_. One hundred and eighteen of

these 306 books have been published since 1945, and 43 of them since

This list, giving only sales figures for the hardbound editions of the

best sellers, is the most comparable in content to the overall

bestseller list in the 1945 edition of this book. In the main that

original list consisted mostly of hardbound best sellers. Some

paperbound editions were included, but when the list was first made,

there were only a scattering of these paperbound editions that could be

included.

The 1895-1955 list has been limited to books selling 750,000 copies or

more in the original edition and in hardbound reprint and in book club

editions. There are 147 titles compared to the 144 books in 1945 which

had sold 500,000 or more. The names of the original publishers and the

dates of original publication in the United States are given. In cases

where the original publisher has gone out of business but the book is

still in print, the name of the present publisher is given, but if the

book itself is out of print, the name of the original publisher is given

whether or not the firm is still in existence.

Many of the older titles from the periods before the two world wars are

still here. Only those on the original list that have not yet sold

750,000 copies are omitted. Many new books of the ’40’s and ’50’s have

been added--58 published in 1945 or later, only 20 of which were

published in 1950 or later. There are 19 authors who have two or more

books on this list. The leaders in number of titles are Frances

Parkinson Keyes with six, Daphne du Maurier, Gene Stratton Porter and

Frank Yerby with five each, and Thomas B. Costain and Harold Bell Wright

with three each.

BEST SELLERS

1895-1955

_Paperbound_

This third list of best sellers, 1895-1955, includes only books which

have sold one million or more copies in paper-covered editions. No

hardcover sales are included in the totals. There are 173 titles on the

list, most of which have sold at prices ranging from 25 cents to 50

cents. The publishers named are the publishers of the paperbound

editions. In some cases these books are “originals” that is, they were

first issued in paper covers, and not as reprints of hardcover editions.

Books in this category are indicated by “(orig)” after the publisher’s

name. In a very few cases a book was issued by its original publisher in

both paper and hardcover editions. In that case only the sales of the

paperbound edition are given.

The most recent development in the paperbound field has been the

establishment of reprint series by the regular trade publishers, most of

them offering their backlist titles at reduced prices in the new format.

The substance of most of these series is more serious from the literary

standpoint than the usual offerings of the regular paperbound

publishers. One of the good outlets for these paperbound series is the

college bookstores whose readers find special books they may have long

wished to own but have not been able to afford at their original prices,

and also books that may have been out of print in their clothbound

editions. This sort of enterprise has been so successful that more and

more publishers have embarked upon it.

Some reprint publishers have always issued paperbound books of serious

nature, notably New American Library with its Mentor Books, Penguin

Books which imports English editions to large extent, and Pocket Books,

which, from its start, has issued dictionaries, classics, and manuals,

such as the one that heads the following list. Titles in the well-known

Modern Library have only recently appeared in paper as well as in cloth,

as have the books in the Viking Portable Library. One of the first trade

publishers to enter this paperbound field was Doubleday & Company with

Anchor Books, a line of paperbound reprints and originals for which

Doubleday won the Carey-Thomas Award for creative publishing in 1955.

Knopf’s Vintage Books have gone over the million mark in sales.

Doubleday’s Image Books, a paper-back series of Catholic works, when

only its first few titles were available, sold over half a million

copies in six months.

An example of another type of paperbound publishing is that of Oceana

Publications and its Legal Almanac Series. These are original works of

several hundred pages, most of them issued in both cloth and paper

bindings. This series specializes in one subject, in this case, law in

simplified form for the layman. Forty volumes of the Legal Almanac

Series have exceeded a million in total sales. The first volume in the

series, _Marriage and Divorce_, has sold over 100,000 copies. There are

also numerous other publishers specializing in issuing paperbound books

on certain subjects. Many of these books are of the “how-to” and

“fix-it-yourself” type, such as those issued by Fawcett Publications,

the Lane Publishing Company, Arco Publishing Company and Maco Magazine

Corporation.

BEST SELLER SUBJECTS

BEST SELLER SUBJECTS

Many individuals, groups and organizations are interested in finding out

what the best sellers are in certain general categories. I have tried to

cover in this section the subjects most frequently asked about. Sales

figures for cook books are without doubt those most often sought.

Religion perhaps comes next (the individual title most asked about above

all other books is the Bible). There is interest in the sales of mystery

and detective stories, and, to a less extent, in “westerns” and

juveniles. Questions about reference books are usually concerned with

their quality. Their standing over the years, implying frequent

revisions (although number of editions is not mentioned here unless

there has been a change of title) is reflected in sales figures. The

same statement is applicable to subscription volumes and sets.

“Novelties” are included because of the stir they often make although

they are usually short-lived. The reverse is true of poetry. One of the

most honored of the creative arts over the centuries, its sales in the

short span of time and place covered here, have been comparatively

small.

COOKERY

Probably more questions are asked in the field of best sellers about the

Bible, next about cook books. All sorts of people and business

organizations ask what the best-selling cook books are and how many

copies of cook books are published and sold. Most interested are the

magazines, especially those designed for the woman at home, and next the

producers of radio and TV programs. It is no coincidence that, next to

the Bible and books on religious subjects, cook books have sold the best

and the most consistently in the non-fiction field since 1895. It is

quite apparent from a study of the best seller lists that only the need

for advice on feeding the spirit surpasses the need for advice--and

books--on feeding the body.

Paradoxically, cook books are seldom seen on the bookstore reports that

appear weekly, monthly, and annually in newspapers and magazines. When a

new, especially salable, and well-publicized cook book is brought out or

when a new edition of an older, standard cook book is published, it does

appear on these lists for a short time. Booksellers are not averse to

listing standard books that sell year in and year out on their reports,

but such reports, dominated as they would be by the Bible, dictionaries,

cook books and other standard books would become monotonous. What

periodical would care to publish them?

Figures on the number of new cook books published annually have been

compiled by the _Publishers’ Weekly_ only since 1952. Cook books have

always been included in the category of domestic science, or, more

recently, home economics. This is one of the smallest categories. In

1955 it comprised 255 titles out of 12,589 new books and new editions

published in the United States. Though small, it is one of the most

important in the book trade and therefore for the general public. It is

quite likely that the number of poor cook books in that 255 is

infinitesimal in proportion to the number of poor novels in the 2073

issued in 1955.

Nearly every cook book is planned to meet a particular need or some

special taste. They vary from the general compendium for the household

and from quantity cook books such as those for large institutions to

little volumes concerned solely with cooking such specialties as

mushrooms or omelettes. There are national and regional cook books,

collections of the favorite recipes of well-known persons, books on

various techniques such as barbecuing or using an electric mixer, and

books for people on every kind of diet. Many of the new cook books and

the new editions of standard books have resulted from the many new

methods of cookery and the new ways of packaging foods developed during

recent years. The number of new books and new editions has remained

fairly steady during the past four years: 102 in 1952; 86 in 1953; 77 in

1954; and 91 in 1955.

The leading best seller among cook books, the _Better Homes and Gardens

Cook Book_ is also one of the top best sellers in all fields. It ranks

fourth in the overall list of best sellers during the past sixty years,

first in the list of best sellers in hardbound editions only. The top

ten on the cookbook best seller list are mostly the standard

encyclopedic type that are reissued from time to time in new editions

embodying new culinary methods. They range in age from the old standby,

familiarly called “Fannie Farmer” or the “Boston Cook Book” to the _Good

and Easy Cook Book_ of 1954. A number of the books are sponsored by

women’s magazines or by food companies.

Included among cook books are the closely allied diet books. They appear

farther down the list, as do also more specialty cook books. Mabel

Claire’s _Modern Cook Book_ has been sold throughout the United States

through department stores under the name of each particular store, viz.

_Smith’s Cook Book_. Next in age to “Fannie Farmer” among the cook books

which have sold over half a million copies is _Diet and Health with Key

to the Calories_. A sensational seller in the 20’s, it appeared on the

annual lists of 1922 and 1923, rose to top place in non-fiction in both

1924 and 1925, and appeared for the fifth year on the 1926 list. _Look

Younger Live Longer_ was third on the best seller list of 1950, first in

1951. One cook book not listed here because its sales records are no

longer available was a wartime best seller of 1917, _Better Meals for

Less Money_.

DETECTION AND MYSTERY

Almost a condensed version of the overall best seller list is this

record of the best sellers in detection and mystery, so close to the top

of all best sellers are these books by Mickey Spillane and Erle Stanley

Gardner, which of course lead in their own field. This is the biggest

list both in number of titles and in total sales of all in the best

seller special subjects section. There are 112 books which have sold

half a million copies or more on this list, and 56 of them are by Erle

Stanley Gardner and/or A. A. Fair, the pen name he uses for his stories

which feature the detectives Bertha Cool and Donald Lam. Next in

numerical representation is Ellery Queen, with 29 titles, including one

appearing here under the pen name of Barnaby Ross. But Mickey Spillane

leads all the rest in sales of individual titles. He has written only

seven books, but they are the top seven on the list.

The original dates of publication are included and the original

publishers except when books are out of print in their original

editions, in which case the name of the present reprint publisher is

given. The dates range from 1908 when Mary Roberts Rinehart’s _The

Circular Staircase_ (in 1920 turned into a hit play, _The Bat_, and

later into a movie) was first published, up to 1954 and _The Glass

Village_ by Ellery Queen.

I have not attempted to draw any distinguishing lines inside the

detection and mystery field. Everything is included which would fall

into the general classification--detection, spy stories, pursuit

stories, psychological mysteries, gangster tales, romantic mysteries,

and stories of violent action. The list illustrates well the radical

changes that have occurred in public taste in this field, changes which

can be considered greater than in any other single subject of creative

writing.

Probably the earliest American best seller (outside of Edgar Allan Poe’s

mystery tales and their big sales did not occur in his lifetime) was

_The Leavenworth Case_ by Anna Katharine Green. It was published in 1878

and has sold over half a million copies. Louis Vance, Meredith

Nicholson, Sir Arthur Conan Doyle and Mary Roberts Rinehart were authors

who appeared regularly on all the annual best seller lists after the

turn of the century. In the late ’20’s S. S. Van Dine created Philo

Vance, a new type of detective. Through the ’20’s and ’30’s Agatha

Christie was considered the favorite of highly literate detective story

readers. Many of her books appear on this list of best sellers, among

them the one that made her famous, The _Murder of Roger Ackroyd_. _The

Thin Man_ came along in 1934, introducing a new sort of writing in

detective fiction. This story, of swift action and terse phrasing, was

the starting point of a new school, the hard-boiled mystery, from which

has emerged the sex-and-guts detective of the ’50’s.

In an article in the _Saturday Review_ called _A Cosmic View of the

Private Eye_, John Paterson writes in praise of the hardboiled detective

as evolved by Hammett and Chandler, of the change from “the

‘transcendant’ detective of an older, safely established order to one in

a bewildering world of disintegrated values.... He is, in the final

analysis, the apotheosis of the everyman of good will who, alienated by

the values of his time, seeks desperately and mournfully to live without

shame, to live without compromise to his integrity.”

In 1946 a select group of readers of _The Pleasures of Publishing_, a

bi-weekly publication of the Columbia University Press, cast their votes

for Dorothy Sayers as their favorite detective story writer. In order of

choice, following her, were John Dickson Carr (Carter Dickson), Erle

Stanley Gardner, A. Conan Doyle, Agatha Christie, Ngaio Marsh, Raymond

Chandler, Dashiell Hammett, Manning Coles and Rex Stout. A Gallup Poll,

in 1950, in which 53 per cent of those questioned were college

graduates, showed Erle Stanley Gardner as their selection for the best

mystery writer of all time.

In the early 1950’s Mickey Spillane’s seven books featuring the ruthless

detective Mike Hammer led all others in sales of individual titles. As

far as total sales of all his books go, Spillane is not up to Gardner.

His sales are about 27,000,000; the sales of all Gardner’s books,

including Canadian sales, amount to over 86,000,000 copies. Erle Stanley

Gardner must be considered, then, the most popular writer of detective

stories in this country, in fact the most popular twentieth century

writer of fiction. He is definitely not of the hard-boiled school; he

presents a deductive problem usually resolved in a dramatic courtroom

scene starring Perry Mason, the lawyer-detective. Perry Mason is not the

rough and ready type. In fact he is so gentlemanly that the suggestion

has been made that it would be an interesting experiment to send the

frustrated Della Street, Mason’s secretary, and the frustrated Archie

Goodwin, whose romantic episodes are always being interrupted by his

boss, Nero Wolfe, off on a weekend together in Atlantic City.

The first Perry Mason mystery, _The Case of the Velvet Claws_, was

published in 1933. It was dictated to a stenographer in three and

one-half days. Nor has Mr. Gardner let down since. He is a prodigious

writer. In 1956 the publication of his fiftieth Perry Mason story was

celebrated. In addition he has written over 30 other books including the

A. A. Fair mysteries, a series featuring the “D.A.” as detective, and

three books of non-fiction--over 80 books in 23 years.

When Mickey Spillane’s fifth thriller, _The Big Kill_, was published in

its paperbound edition, something of a record in print orders was made.

The printing consisted of 2,500,000 copies and at the same time new

printings of one million copies each of his first four books were made.

Although Spillane is the mystery phenomenon of the 1950’s he must be

considered only third in line in popularity of American mystery story

writers of all time. Ellery Queen, pseudonym of two men, Frederic Dannay

and Manfred B. Lee, estimates the sale of his works at 35,000,000 plus a

foreign sale of from 15 to 20 million. Queen, the son of a New York City

police official, is always the hero-detective of the books that bear

his name as author. His two originators have published 32 books since

their first in 1929, _The Roman Hat Mystery_. They have also written a

number of juvenile mysteries under the name of Ellery Queen, Jr.

Probably next in order of popularity with American detective story

readers is the English writer, Agatha Christie. Her detectives, of the

deductive school, are principally Hercule Poirot and the quiet little

Englishwoman, Miss Marple. Running close to the Christie novels in sales

are those by Brett Halliday, featuring the man of action, Mike Shayne,

tough-fisted and with an eye for blondes--or brunettes.

Figures for total sales of the output of other popular writers have been

estimated mainly from the reports of the paperbound publishers, who are

responsible for the big sales in this field: over seven million, Richard

S. Prather; over six million, Wade Miller, John Dickson Carr (Carter

Dickson), and William Ard; over five million, Raymond Chandler, Leslie

Charteris, E. Phillips Oppenheim, George Harmon Coxe; over four million,

Mary Roberts Rinehart (her total sales, including hardbound books are

much greater than this. She has written well over 50 books, but not all

of them mysteries.); over three million, Georges Simenon, Dashiell

Hammett, F. Van Wyck Mason, Rex Stout and Helen Reilly; over two

million, Frances and Richard Lockridge, James Hadley Chase, Bruno

Fischer, Leslie Ford, Helen McCloy, Mignon Eberhart, Henry Kane, Edgar

Wallace, Bill Ballinger, Sax Rohmer, Ed Lacy, and Peter Cheyney.

Individual detective stories which appeared on the annual best seller

lists but not on the overall mystery best seller list, some of their

sales figures not available now, are, chronologically, _The Hound of the

Baskervilles_, by A. Conan Doyle (100,569 sold), _The House of a

Thousand Candles_, by Meredith Nicholson (289,944 sold), _The Brass

Bowl_, by Louis J. Vance (184,071 sold), _The Port of Missing Men_, by

Meredith Nicholson (150,429 sold), _The Black Bag_, by Louis J. Vance

(35,247 sold), _The Man in Lower Ten_, by Mary Roberts Rinehart (113,360

sold), _The Window at the White Cat_, by Mary Roberts Rinehart (152,002

sold), _The Pawns Count_, by E. Phillips Oppenheim, _The Greene Murder

Case_, by S. S. Van Dine, _The Bishop Murder Case_, by S. S. Van Dine,

and _The Door_, by Mary Roberts Rinehart.

JUVENILES

Statistics on best sellers in the children’s book field are more

difficult to assemble accurately and consistently than in any other. The

following list of 75 books is an attempt to record the individual

titles, published in the United States since 1895, that have sold a

quarter of a million copies or more.

There are many of the oldest favorites and classics on the list of early

best sellers, those published before 1895. As these books have gone out

of copyright they have been issued by various publishers, perhaps newly

illustrated or in re-edited or condensed versions. It becomes impossible

to collect exact statistics for all these scattered versions, even for

some published later than 1895. Many of the original publishers have

gone out of existence and even some modern publishers do not have sales

records that go back very far. When there have been many publishers and

many versions of the same story, it is an all but impossible task to

assemble all the sales records, hence the absence of such favorites as

_The Tale of Peter Rabbit_, by Beatrix Potter. Modern versions of old

favorites are not included in the list unless one specific volume,

perhaps with a special illustrator, has accurate sales records, like

_The Real Mother Goose_ and _Walt Disney’s Uncle Remus_. Inexpensive

paperbound books in series are not included.

Juvenile fiction has a disconcerting habit of changing its audience.

Some of these best sellers like _Freckles_ and _Penrod_ started their

careers as adult fiction. Others were launched as juveniles and have

since added an adult audience, like the A. A. Milne books. The majority

of the books on this list were, however, planned for the youngest group

of juvenile readers.

A unique venture in selling to the older age group of children has been

the Teen Age Book Club, started over ten years ago. Its books are

paperbound, are sold at 25 and 35 cents, and are usually reprints, many

of them of adult titles suitable for teen age reading. In the school

year 1954-55 the Club sold 2,800,000 paperback books to student members

and over 10,000,000 in the past six years. The club’s top sellers in the

1954-1955 period were _Magnificent Obsession_ by Lloyd C. Douglas and

_The Real Story of Lucille Ball_ by Eleanor Harris.

The top sellers on the following list of best sellers might be said to

sell to captive audiences. They are not regular trade books, but are

“musts” for Boy Scouts and Girl Scouts. Their very large sales totals,

covering yearly distribution, merit places for them here. Additionally

the Boy Scouts of America has given me some typical figures for some of

their other books. These are for the year 1955; _Wolf Cub Scout Books_,

970,000; _Bear Cub Scout Books_, 750,000; _Lion Webelos Cub Scout

Books_, 595,000.

There are huge quantities of children’s books sold which can only be

indicated here. The 10-cent to one dollar stores, department stores, and

newsstands are the chief outlets for these books which include coloring

books, cut-out books, and paper-doll books, for example, as well as

versions of the classics and juvenile fiction. One of the big sections

of this type of production and sale of children’s books is comprised of

“series.” Children latch on to a favorite character and follow him or

her through every imaginable adventure in volume after volume. Total

estimates of sales for some of these series are available. Not all of

these listed just below fall, however, into the class of those just

described. The _Landmark Books_ and _First Book Series_, for example,

have their greatest sales through regular bookstore outlets.

_Little Golden Books_, originated in 1942, have sold 285,000,000 in the

United States and Canada.

The _Frank Merriwell_ series, by Burt L. Standish, which flourished in

the ’20’s had an estimated sale of 126,000,000.

The 20 _Uncle Arthur Bedtime Stories_, by Arthur S. Maxwell, begun in

1927, have sold 19,601,979 copies.

The _Golden Play Books_, 15 cents each, have sold 10,000,000.

_Miss Frances’ Ding Dong School Books_ have sold over 10,000,000 since

1953. Their publicity is tied in with Miss Frances’ TV program for

children, and they sell at 25 cents each.

The _Burgess Animal Books_, by Thornton Burgess, have sold well over

8,000,000 copies.

With over 4,000,000 sales are the _Bedtime Story Book_ series by

Thornton Burgess and the _Childhood of Famous Americans_ series.

In the 3,000,000 group are the _Landmark Books_ with over 75 titles, the

_Golden Nature Guides_, the _Pollyanna Glad Books_, 15 titles, the _Twin

Books_ of Lucy Fitch Perkins, 24 of them, and the _Raggedy Ann Books_,

originated by Johnny Gruelle in 1918. The _Raggedy Ann Books_ have also

had many by-products in the way of cut-out books, mechanical books, and

the Raggedy Ann and Raggedy Andy dolls.

Sixty titles of the _First Book Series_, edited by Helen Hoke Watts,

have sold 2,265,903 copies.

The famous _Elsie Dinsmore_ books by Martha Finley have sold well over

2,000,000. There are 28 titles, the first published in 1868.

Around the 2,000,000 mark is the _Little Colonel_ series by Annie

Fellows Johnston.

Twenty titles in the _Petersham Story Book_ series have sold 1,728,000;

Walter Farley’s _Black Stallion_ books, ten of them, have sold

1,250,000; the _Andrew Lang Fairy Tale Series_ has sold 1,268,367, and

11 volumes of the _Five Little Peppers_ series by Margaret Sidney have

sold 1,415,978 through one publisher since 1937. The first book in this

series was published in 1880, and the total sale is, of course, much

greater.

In the over one million group are the _Colby Books_; the _Real Book

Series_; the _Oz_ books of L. Frank Baum; the _Magic Talking Books_,

with records on their covers; the _Seahorse Fun Books_ at $1 each; the

_Teen-Age Library_; 21 volumes of the _Young Readers Bookshelf_; the

_Buddy Books_; and such older stories as the _Ruth Fielding_, _Betty

Gordon_, and _Motor Boys_ books.

Illustrating the pull of comic books (not magazines) are the _Classics

Illustrated Series_ and the _Classics Illustrated Junior Series_. These

two lines were designed to present worth-while educational subjects in

the comic book format which children like so much. Well over a million

copies of the books in each series have been sold. The publisher of some

of the older comic books, predecessors of today’s big sellers, report a

total sale of over 30,000,000 of such titles as _Bringing Up Father_,

_Mutt and Jeff_, _Little Orphan Annie_ and _Dick Tracy_.

Books distributed only through the low-price stores are those issued by

such firms as the Whitman Publishing Company and the Saalfield

Publishing Company. The Saalfield _Play Books_, mostly coloring and

paper-doll books, which sell for 25 cents and under, have a lifetime

each of from six months to two years. They are always issued in first

printings of at least a quarter of a million. No records of individual

books are kept. During the 1955 Davy Crockett craze, Grosset & Dunlap

sold about two million copies of three Davy Crockett books, including

one million of _The Picture Story of Davy Crockett_ and 600,000 of _The

Davy Crockett Coloring Book_.

NOVELTIES AND GAMES

Periodically, a new fad hits the American public. It may be a dance or a

song, a slang phrase or a game. Sometimes such a fad is opportunely made

the basis of a book that sells until fickle taste turns elsewhere.

Sometimes a new brand of book creates the fad as happened with the

_Cross Word Puzzle Books_, which perhaps should head the list of best

sellers in this field, but have been excluded because they constitute a

series.

Cross word puzzles had been enjoyed by a select group of fans for some

years before 1934 when the then new and untried firm of Simon and

Schuster made a collection of the puzzles that had been appearing in the

New York _World_. The book was compiled by Prosper Buranelli, F. Gregory

Hartswick and Margaret Petherbridge, and it had the added innovation of

a pencil that went along with each book in a slot on the binding. The

rest is history. Not only did the books themselves create a host of

cross word puzzle fans but cross word puzzles have continued ever since

as popular newspaper features. They have developed into “Little

Foolers,” diagramless puzzles and _Double-Crostics_ for super experts.

Simon and Schuster alone had published 73 of the _Cross Word Puzzle

Books_ through 1955 and 35 _Double-Crostics_. Two million one hundred

thousand _Cross Word Puzzle Books_ were sold through 1955, 200,000 of

the _Double-Crostics_ books. The editorship of the _Cross Word Puzzle

Books_ has been continued by Margaret Petherbridge Farrar, that of the

_Double-Crostics_ initiated by Elizabeth S. Kingsley and continued by

Doris Nash Wortman.

There are not nearly so many _Believe It Or Not_ books as _Cross Word

Puzzle Books_. This was originally also a newspaper feature, conceived

and drawn by Robert L. Ripley, who capitalized on the _Believe It or

Not_ fad not only in books and newspaper syndication but in shows,

exhibits and many other ways. _Boners_ and _Ask Me Another_, great fads

in their day, were also published in successive volumes after the first.

Humor, usually in a ribald vein, is dominant on this list of novelty

best sellers. There are many cartoon and joke books on it, and pictorial

humor like the _Zoo_ books of Clare Barnes, Jr. _The Specialist_,

monologue by a onetime vaudeville headliner, continues to sell and has

been published in ten other countries. _Dennis_, which started as a

newspaper picture feature, has probably only started on its best-selling

career, with its first two volumes listed here. Another popular cartoon

feature reproduced in a number of paperbound books is Walt Kelly’s

_Pogo_. About 1,600,000 of the Pogo books have been sold, though no

individual title has sold enough to win a place among these 19 books

that have sold over 275,000 copies.

There are eight manuals of games making up the second list of more

serious game books, half of them bridge books. Bridge has always been a

best-selling subject from the time of Milton C. Work and auction bridge

even before the ’20’s. Robert F. Foster’s auction and contract bridge

books sold over 1,000,000 copies. The late ’20’s and ’30’s when contract

bridge replaced auction was the Culbertson heyday. In the ’40’s and

’50’s bridge instruction books increased in number and continued to sell

well. Outstanding in the ’50’s are those by Charles Goren, who estimates

the sales of his seven titles at over two million. Canasta book sales

hit their high in 1949. The publisher of _Invitation to Chess_, by

Irving Chernev and Kenneth Harkness, believes it to be the best-selling

book on chess with 74,058 copies sold.

PAMPHLETS

A very special branch of all book sales consists of pamphlets. They are

probably the least publicized and the least important to the retail

bookseller of all printed matter. Yet their volume of production and

sales is very large. The latest available statistics are those issued in

1949 by the Bureau of Census. The figures are for the year 1947. In that

year 402,290,000 pamphlets were sold with a value of $20,656,000. The

comparable figures for books were 487,216,000 sold with a value of

$435,124,000. In that year there were 9,182 new books and new editions

of books published. No complete record of individual pamphlet titles has

been kept, but it seems likely from the census figures that there are

more new pamphlets than new books produced each year. The great mass of

pamphlets issued is probably attributable to the Government Printing

Office, to State printing offices, and to universities.

A pamphlet is customarily defined as paperbound printed matter of 64

pages or less. Usually the subject is very specialized. Pamphleteering

usually means printing or writing material to be printed in support of a

cause or to denounce a wrong. This type of pamphlet publishing makes up

a very small part of twentieth century output, although it does exist.

Typical twentieth century pamphleteering is informative rather than

crusading in purpose. Among popular pamphlets of this sort are the Town

Hall Meeting and Chicago Round Table discussions on the air of current

topics, which are regularly printed and distributed. An original series

is _Public Affairs Pamphlets_ issued by the Public Affairs Committee, a

nonprofitmaking organization which, in its own words, has “revived and

modernized pamphleteering as a primary method of conveying important

knowledge to a wide and diversified audience.” In its nineteen years of

existence this organization has sold over 22 million pamphlets at 25

cents each. Its pamphlets are often co-sponsored and distributed

through other national organizations such as the American Red Cross.

Many are used in large corporations’ employee reading racks. Some are

sold on newsstands.

Public Affairs Committee’s own best sellers are _What About Girls_,

1,188,306; _Races of Mankind_, 966,997; _Blood’s Magic for All_,

747,471; _Facing the Facts About Cancer_, 683,953; _Epilepsy, The Ghost

Is Out of the Closet_, 604,800; and _Building Your Marriage_, 522,031.

The Government Printing Office is probably the leading producer of

printed matter in the United States. Established by Congress in 1895,

the year the first best seller lists were made, it presently sells about

47,000,000 items each year and distributes some 78,000,000 without

charge, principally to libraries. Profits, which have amounted to more

than $1,500,000 annually since 1950, are turned over to the Treasury

Department. All the material is authored by government departments and

agencies.

The Superintendent of Documents has supplied the titles and sales

figures of some representative best sellers issued by the Government

Printing Office, from which the following titles have been selected.

They range in price from five cents to $1.50: _Infant Care_ (Children’s

Bureau), 10,012,934; _Prenatal Care_ (Children’s Bureau), 4,959,321;

_The Child from One to Six_ (Children’s Bureau), 3,893,329; _Your

Federal Income Tax_, 1,838,516; _Your Child from Six to Twelve_

(Children’s Bureau), 1,415,432; _First Aid_ (Bureau of Mines), 771,410;

_Modern Miracle Men_ (Senate Document), 685,982; _Aircraft Powerplant

Handbook_ (C.A.A.), 562,279; _Pilots Weather Handbook_, 520,194; _What

You Should Know About Biological Warfare_ (Federal Civil Defense

publication), 480,246. Other varied subjects are _Lee Mansion_, 181,511;

_Window Curtains_, 69,722; _Pointers on Making Good Lawns_, 207,845;

_Making Cellars Dry_, 153,367; _Prospecting for Uranium_, 330,795.

POETRY AND DRAMA

Poetry is the stepchild of the best sellers. Individual books of poems

seldom appear on the annual best seller lists. In the list following,

_John Brown’s Body_, _The White Cliffs_ (second on the 1941 list), and

_I’m a Stranger Here Myself_, are among the eleven books of poetry that

have made the annual lists during sixty years. The two Pocket Books were

sold through the outlets for paperbound books, sales of which are never

reflected in the bookstore best seller lists.

The other books which have appeared on the annual best seller lists in

the course of sixty years, and for only some of which sales figures are

obtainable, are chronologically, _Poems of Alan Seeger_, 1917; _Rhymes

of a Red Cross Man_, by Robert W. Service, which headed both the 1917

and 1918 lists; _Over Here_, by Edgar Guest, 1918; _Treasury of War

Poetry_, by G. H. Clark, 1918 (67,860 sold); _In Flanders Fields_, by

John McCrae, 1919 (17,000 sold); _The Years Between_, by Rudyard

Kipling, 1919 (35,805 sold); _The Light of Faith_, by Edgar Guest, 1926

and; _Fatal Interview_, by Edna St. Vincent Millay, 1931 (64,450 sold).

It is obvious from these annual and overall lists that it is war poetry

which has had the popular bookstore sale.

Estimates of the total sales of all the works of modern poets put Edgar

Guest and Robert W. Service in the lead. There have been, however,

substantial bookstore sales of individual titles and collected works of

poets not previously mentioned, among them, Robert Frost, Edwin

Arlington Robinson, T. S. Eliot, and Carl Sandburg. Poetry anthologies

are also standard volumes sales-wise in the stores, such as those edited

by Louis Untermeyer and Oscar Williams.

Drama

Plays in book form have a small but devoted audience. In sixty years

only three books of plays have appeared on the annual best seller lists,

none on the overall lists. The three books are _Three Plays_ by Eugène

Brieux, in 1912, _Saint Joan_ by Bernard Shaw in 1924, and _Strange

Interlude_ by Eugene O’Neill, a best seller of 1928, which sold between

100,000 and 150,000 copies. More recently, in 1950, _The Lady’s Not for

Burning_ by Christopher Fry has appeared on bookstore lists, although

its sales were not large enough to place it on any list of the year. New

American Library has announced that its sales, in paperbound editions,

make Tennessee Williams the leading modern playwright in terms of book

sales. About 750,000 copies of his plays have been sold in the 25-cent

editions. A recent development in the selling of plays in book form is

the Fireside Theatre. This is a book club started in 1949, which selects

plays in book form for its membership. In the past few years its average

circulation of individual plays has been 11,000 copies. Most popular of

its selections have been _Teahouse of the August Moon_ by John Patrick,

_Bell, Book and Candle_ by John Van Druten, _Wonderful Town_ by Jerome

Chodorov and Joseph Fields, and _The Caine Mutiny Court-Martial_ by

Herman Wouk. All through this book are a great many books from which

plays and motion pictures have been made.

REFERENCE

Reference books, strictly speaking, are the dictionaries, encyclopedias,

atlases which comprise the first list in this section. They are chiefly

concerned with words and language usage or are compendia of facts.

There are almost as many editions of dictionaries as there are of the

Bible. In many cases it is impossible to collect sales statistics for

specific volumes. Unfortunately this situation results in some notable

omissions from this list. The G. & C. Merriam Company, one of the oldest

dictionary publishers, established in 1831, was the first publisher of

the Webster dictionary after Noah Webster himself. This first

Merriam-Webster dictionary was issued in 1847, and has gone through many

revisions. The principal twentieth-century editions are the _Webster’s

New International Dictionary_ and _Webster’s Collegiate Dictionary_. The

publisher will not release sales figures, but it is evident that they

run into many millions. The only figures that have been available are

scanty, but they give an inkling of the rest. This is the four and

one-half million copy sale of _Webster’s New Collegiate Dictionary_

between 1936 and 1949 when the sixth edition was issued.

Other large publishers of dictionaries have given some general figures

about their sales. The World Publishing Company, with eight separate

dictionaries, has averaged a total of about 1,800,000 copies a year for

the past five years. All the Funk & Wagnalls dictionaries have sold well

over the half-million mark.

One of the best-known American reference works is _Robert’s Rules of

Order_. First published in 1876, it has sold close to a million and

one-half copies. Another important reference work originally published

in the nineteenth century is Bartlett’s _Familiar Quotations_. In the

years 1937-1955 during which it has been a Little, Brown & Company

publication, that firm has sold 807,632 copies.

Technical Reference Works

Lines of demarcation in the field of technical reference works are not

well defined. In some cases it is a matter of opinion whether a

technical book is a reference work or a textbook; it may be both.

Probably the only large area of book sales not covered, for many

reasons, in the present book is that of textbooks. Soaring enrollments

in elementary and high schools and in colleges and estimates of future

school population at an even greater increase than that of the past

decade make textbooks among the books most in demand and most necessary

for the future. There are not many textbooks that have, so far, sold in

the millions. Though they are often revised they also frequently become

out of date comparatively soon after publication. One of the problems of

defining textbook sales has been expressed by a college textbook

publisher who writes, “ ... it is not possible for a publisher to know

how many copies of one of his best sellers have actually been sold.

Since 1942 we have sold about 100,000 copies of a literature

anthology--but at least five times as many sales of this title have been

made through the second-hand facilities on college campuses all over the

country.... Although not every college student sells every one of his

textbooks, the overwhelming majority sell the overwhelming majority of

the books that they buy new and as many of the second-hand volumes as

they can dispose of. For these reasons there is often little

relationship between the number of books that a college publisher

_sells_ of a given title in a given year and the total number of copies

of this same title that are sold during that year.”

Fields of textbook publishing, vary as well as their sales. For example,

a very different type from the regular elementary or college textbooks

are the publications of Berlitz Publications, Inc., known

internationally for its book and record language teaching methods.

Charles F. Berlitz calls attention to three of the basic Berlitz books,

the _Libro Español_, the _Premier Livre_ and the English _First Book_.

Since 1880 they have sold, respectively, about 4,200,000, 6,100,000, and

5,300,000 copies. One of the oldest American textbook lines, in the

elementary field, is the _McGuffey Readers_, remembered, often fondly,

by those who were school children in the late nineteenth, early

twentieth, centuries. The first _McGuffey Reader_ was published in 1836.

The sales of all have been estimated at over 122,000,000. Even older was

the “blue-black speller,” Noah Webster’s _The American Spelling Book_,

sales of which have been estimated at over 60 million.

The following list has been confined to technical reference works, some

of which may be used as textbooks, but most of which are mainly

purchased for use outside regular educational courses--by craftsmen,

technicians, amateur or would-be technicians, homeowners, and others.

Mention may be made here of the large sales of a series of year books in

a specialized technical field. The Spectator has published nearly 90

yearly editions of the _Life Index_ and _Fire Index_, giving annual

insurance statistics. In the aggregate, 3,600,000 of the _Life Index_

have been sold, and 1,900,000 of the _Fire Index_.
